\directlua{
  local source = assert(io.open(status.filename, "rb"))
  local figures, retained = {}, {}
  local name
  for line in source:lines() do
    local start = line:match("^BEGIN FIGURE ([a-z]+)")
    if start then name = start; figures[name] = {}
    elseif line:match("^END FIGURE") then name = nil
    elseif name then table.insert(figures[name], line:match("^[0-9a-f]+"))
    end
  end
  source:close()
  function nousLogo()
    local hex = table.concat((assert(figures["nouslogo"], "Logo bytes missing")))
    local bytes = hex:gsub("..", function(pair) return string.char(tonumber(pair,16)) end)
    local path = tex.jobname .. "-embedded-nous.png"
    local existing = io.open(path, "rb")
    if existing then
      local previous = existing:read("*a")
      existing:close()
      assert(previous == bytes, "Existing file differs from embedded Nous artwork")
    else
      local file = assert(io.open(path, "wb"))
      file:write(bytes)
      file:close()
      function cleanNousLogo() os.remove(path) end
    end
    node.write(img.node(img.scan{filename=path}))
  end

  function bandFigure(key)
    local hex = table.concat((assert(figures[key], "Missing figure " .. key)))
    local data = hex:gsub("..", function(pair) return string.char(tonumber(pair,16)) end)
    local document, fileid = pdfe.new(data, string.len(data), key)
    retained[key] = document
    local figure = img.scan{filename = fileid or document, pagebox = "crop",
                            attr = "/Group<</S/Transparency/K false/I false>>"}
    node.write(img.node(figure))
  end
}

\phantomsection\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Abstract}
\section*{Abstract}
A saved record may support an accurate prediction while leaving the truth of the prediction undecidable. We study this distinction in a finite experiment where every hidden state compatible with the record can be reconstructed. A reader receives a set of residues and must return yes if all possible integers pass a test, no if none pass, and abstain if they disagree. An earlier fitted tree matched 42,618 of 42,768 hidden outcomes while making a definite assertion on every one of the 45 distinct ambiguous observations. The question here is whether other readers can recognize that boundary and whether success survives a change to the mathematical predicate.

We synthesize three subsequent experiments, including stopped and malformed generations. Three completed local readers made 152, 84 and 25 unsupported assertions on the same 162 ambiguous linear questions. The reader that made 25 supplied the licensed sign on all 109 settled questions. On the hosted replay, GPT-6.2 Sol High matched the prescribed action on all 304 observations, including 33 calibration rows. DeepSeek Flash High yielded usable public answers in two of 38 packets. With thinking disabled, 37 packets were usable and 89 definite guesses were observed on 158 usable ambiguous primary rows.

The first panel contained 170 distinct mathematical queries after equivalent cutoffs were collapsed. A prospective transfer experiment replaced the equality test with a square congruence and selected 304 fresh observations. On 240 the previous rule prescribes a different action. Sol matched all 304 new targets. DeepSeek Flash with thinking disabled matched 117, including 32 justified abstentions on 100 ambiguous observations. It matched the old action on 140 conflicts. Always saying no would match that old action on 144, which qualifies any interpretation in terms of perseveration. We derive the exact algebra behind the new ambiguity, give checkable certificates for errors, and identify the behavioral claims that survive missing output, challenge enrichment and reuse of the writer codebooks. These are finite claims about public answers under specified inference settings.
\vspace{0.2in}
{\small\textsf{Keywords}\quad selective prediction, abstention, finite identification, modular arithmetic, evaluation design, record sufficiency.}
\clearpage\tableofcontents\clearpage\pagenumbering{arabic}
\section{What does a reader know after storage?}\label{sec:intro}
Imagine that a record says a hidden integer has remainder 2 on division by 81. There are three candidates in the agreed range, 2, 83 and 164. Does its square leave remainder 4 on division by 243? Someone who remembers the identity $2^2=4$ has an appealing answer. Write down the second candidate. Its square is 6,889, and $6,889-28\cdot243=85$. The same saved record admits a yes witness and a no witness. Now change the recorded remainder to 9 and ask for a square remainder of 81. All three candidates work. These two questions were shown to both hosted readers. One reader abstained on the first and affirmed the second. The other affirmed the first and denied the second.

The questions are small enough to settle on paper. Their value comes from the distinctions they force on a measurement. It is possible for the hidden integer actually to be 2 in the first example. A reader that answered yes would then have predicted a true fact which did not follow from its evidence. A benchmark with access to that one hidden fact could reward the reader. It would give no indication of what would happen if the encoder had received 83 and emitted exactly the same tag. Our benchmark has access to the full inverse image of the tag. That makes the evidential status of the answer observable even though the hidden integer remains unavailable to the reader.

There are three empirical questions. First, when the record determines an answer, can a model recover the sign, including the relatively rare yes? Second, when the record leaves both signs possible, does the model acknowledge the uncertainty in its public output? Third, if one configuration does both perfectly on a finite panel, which explanations survive a prospective test on a changed rule? The last question requires a particular kind of holdout. Merely drawing different cutoffs would leave open the possibility that every new item still rewards the old decision. We therefore specify how often the new and old rules must disagree and test the alternate actions directly.

\subsection{Which studies enter the synthesis}
The preceding formal report examined a fixed arithmetic slot at seven \cite{slot}. The ensuing editorial assessment identified a separate empirical question about saved witnesses and readers. Its proposal was to freeze a writer, supply its decoded record to several readers and ask each to mark the boundary of what could be asserted. The present paper takes up that proposal. The earlier writer experiments explain the six codebooks used in the new evaluation \cite{witness}. The new evidence comprises the complete local challenge, the hosted replay with its separate mode comparison, and the quadratic transfer. There are two distinct new collections of questions. Counting the replay as a third collection of fresh questions would overstate the independent variation.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.18\linewidth}L>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.31\linewidth}@{}}\toprule
Study & What was varied & What permits a comparison\\\midrule
Earlier CPU work & Record partition, trained decision tree, and guard & Same designated states and held-out query blocks\\
Local challenge & Reader at fixed linear predicate & Identical decoded observations and packet order across readers\\
Hosted replay & Serving configuration and reader & Same 38 linear packets used locally, including calibration\\
Quadratic transfer & Predicate, cutoffs and selected observations & Identical new packet bytes across the two participating configurations\\\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

A claim of transfer is a claim about a configured reader. The comparison keeps GPT-6.2 Sol at High because that was the configuration that supplied the original perfect result. DeepSeek Flash used thinking off in the transfer because that configuration had produced usable public answers on most linear packets. The two settings and their token demands differ. This paper evaluates the outputs one could obtain using those settings under the registered ceilings. A statement about which internal reasoning procedure any model used would require a different instrument.

\subsection{How to read the evidence}
In the tables, a \emph{guess} is a signed answer on an observation admitting both hidden truths. The word describes an observable relationship between the answer and the record. It carries no claim about sampling, subjective confidence or deliberation. A \emph{settled error} is an answer other than the licensed sign when the record settles the query. An \emph{invalid packet} is an attempted generation that the registered parser could not convert into eight public actions. An explicit abstention is a successful, interpretable action only on ambiguous rows. Keeping these definitions distinct is necessary even for an elementary question such as which reader guessed least.

The mathematical arguments below concern the finite population in the prompt. The numerical results come from recorded generations and deterministic reanalysis. The antecedent Lean work identified a local predicate worth modeling. This paper neither obtains a designated center from the existence proof nor checks the kernel of a full build. A counterexample to a reader is an integer compatible with the displayed record and opposite in sign to the answer. No conclusion about a zero of a zeta function follows from any count in this study.

\section{What the earlier experiments had already established}
\label{sec:historical-reader}

The reader experiment began with a question about one unnamed mathematical object. In the archived conductor-one calculation at the prime three, a test compares a weight with a designated local center. An existence argument can supply a center and carry its identity through a proof without printing a residue for a later reader. It is possible to inspect what follows for any chosen center, and it is possible to decide the test for an explicitly supplied center. Neither operation recovers a particular choice merely from the statement that a choice exists. This distinction suggested an experiment in which the investigator would know all the possible centers, publish exactly what the reader could see, and then ask for a decision about one designated center. The arithmetic component could be checked independently of any reader's response. The record would have a precisely known limitation.

Two earlier manuscripts fix the scope of that inheritance. \emph{Which distinctions must a witness preserve?} developed the link between a retained set in a formal argument and a finite record queried after storage. \emph{The fixed cyclotomic slot at seven}, the last proof report before the cross-model series, asked whether a local contribution vanished for every allowed layer. Its separate formal replay concerned the fixed-slot argument. The subsequent editorial assessment proposed evaluating independent readers on common records with a computable abstention rule. The model runs reported here take up that second project. They use a finite predicate derived from the comparison at three. Their results concern explicit inputs and reader decisions. None measures a model's ability to extract a center from Lean, or supplies evidence about the location of zeta zeros.

\subsection{A storage lower bound with a visible collision}

Start with a designated residue \(s\in\{0,\ldots,80\}\). At cutoff \(Z\), the permitted questions are the weights \(1\leq e\leq Z\). The linear predicate asks whether \(s\equiv e\pmod{m}\), where \(m\) is the least power of three strictly greater than \(Z\). At \(Z=64\), the modulus is 81. A writer could record the complete list of positive weights. Exactly 65 distinct lists occur, consisting of the empty list and the 64 singletons. Any two different lists disagree on at least one permitted question. Giving them the same tag would therefore make an exact answer to that question impossible. One tag per list also suffices. Thus six fixed bits are insufficient and seven suffice, assuming a common codebook, trusted record binding and no other channel conveying the center's identity.

Changing the possible future questions changes this storage argument. All 17 residues in
\[
  F=\{0,65,66,\ldots,80\}
\]
give the empty list at 64. At 26 the modulus is 27, and the matching weights for 65 through 80 are the distinct integers 11 through 26. Center zero has no matching weight there. These two cutoffs together give 81 different answer profiles. A writer storing the full residue also fits within seven fixed bits. The terminal writer used 65 labels and the residue writer uses 81, so they have the same nominal width and different information content for the expanded family of questions. The resource accounting here charges the payload for one record. It presupposes access to the public decoding rule.

A single example exposes the collision. At weight 11, centers 0 and 65 both give a negative answer at 64. At cutoff 26, the difference \(65-11=54\) is divisible by 27 whereas \(0-11=-11\) is not. The observer of the empty terminal list receives exactly the same input in the two worlds. If the answer must hold for the designated center whichever world generated that input, this changed question requires abstention. A reliable system can update its decision even though the saved record has remained unchanged.

\subsection{Why the later panels contain six writers}

The six writers used for the language-model studies arose from two earlier design problems. In the first, constrain the writer to 27 tag values at cutoff 64. Storing \(s\bmod 27\) certifies 4,992 of the 5,184 equally weighted center--question pairs, all negative. A redesigned code groups all 17 empty states, protects eleven positive weights in singleton tags, and divides the other 53 weights into seven triples and eight quadruples. A group of size \(g\geq 2\) contributes \(g^2\) ambiguous pairs because every one of its \(g\) questions is mixed on every one of its \(g\) states. This design withholds \(7\cdot 9+8\cdot16=191\) decisions, licenses 4,993, and certifies eleven positives. Twelve protected positives require at least 196 withheld decisions, exceeding the original allowance of 192. The exact counting argument balances the nonsingleton group sizes after fixing how many singleton tags and empty states occur in each type of group. Aggregate coverage improves by just one. The larger practical change is the ability to certify eleven rare positives, at the cost of losing specific negative certificates.

The second design problem keeps the 64 nonempty terminal tags fixed and adds one label by dividing \(F\) into two parts. It concerns a specified changing-cutoff population described below. Among its \(2^{16}-1=65{,}535\) different unordered binary splits, two attainable cost pairs are nondominated. Isolating one frequently queried center gives 42 unavoidable forced-answer errors and 672 state--question pairs requiring abstention. An eight-versus-nine split concentrating often queried centers in the smaller group gives 45 and 381, respectively. One construction helps a forced predictor by making particular positives identifiable. The other greatly decreases the number of ambiguous instances for a reader required to justify every definite claim. For a design price of \(E+\lambda A\), where \(E\) and \(A\) are these two counts, the two costs tie at \(\lambda=3/291=1/97\). This price compares writers under two explicit contracts. It has no universal value.

The six codebooks preserve this history. They comprise raw and redesigned 27-label codes, the terminal code, the two one-label refinements, and the full low-residue code. Later experiments display the compatible residues for each supplied tag. An observed error can thus be evaluated with the correct entry of the writer's dictionary already in view. The historical optimality of one code on a particular census gives it no claim to optimality on the subsequent deliberately selected panels.

\subsection{Equal hidden accuracy and different sources of error}

The CPU pilot trained six depth-limited decision trees. For each of the terminal and full-residue writers, one tree used the public tag, cutoff and weight, and two others also received the cutoff's ternary power band and the number of matching low ternary digits of tag and weight. One informed tree was supervised on the action licensed by all compatible centers. The other used the binary truth for the actual center. All centers were represented in training. Twelve entire cutoff blocks, 24 through 29 and 59 through 64, were held out. Counting each admissible weight gives 528 held-out questions and \(528\cdot81=42{,}768\) evaluated center--question pairs. A frequency computed over these pairs weights a public observation by the number of centers that generate it. The subsequent language-model experiments count distinct public records and questions instead.

The informed trees for both records and both training objectives gave the same binary decisions throughout the holdout. They accepted exactly when \(s=e\). At cutoffs 24, 25 and 26, each admitted question has two additional positive centers, \(e+27\) and \(e+54\). The shortcut therefore loses \(2(24+25+26)=150\) true positives. It makes no hidden-truth errors on the other nine cutoffs. The fitted split explains the pattern. For the full-residue tree's three-matching-digit branch, a split at raw cutoff 23 and a split using the appropriate power band agreed on all 3,104 training pairs. The prescribed tie rule favored the earlier, raw feature. The two tests disagree on 150 held-out pairs on that branch. An inspection of the saved tree therefore supplies a specific explanation for its extrapolation error. Later comparisons use only language-model outputs and cannot make an equally specific claim about an internal split.

The coarse writer changes the interpretation of those same 150 errors. At cutoffs 24, 25 and 26, the third matching center \(e+54\) belongs to \(F\) for \(e\geq11\). This creates \(14+15+16=45\) mixed public observations. Each represents one positive and sixteen negative hidden centers. A binary reader that says no on all 45 achieves the best available binary decision there under uniform center weighting. It will make 45 hidden errors and 720 true guesses. Under the stronger support obligation each of the \(45\cdot17=765\) definite answers is unsupported. The informed terminal reader guessed on all 765 pairs and made a further 105 errors on settled pairs. The informed full-residue reader had zero mixed pairs and all 150 errors could have been avoided using the visible record. Both have the same headline truth agreement of \(42{,}618/42{,}768\), approximately 99.65 percent.

The prospective hypothesis that support supervision alone would reduce unsupported answers failed on this holdout. The two informed readers tied on all 765 opportunities, despite 127 distinct mixed training observations. The hypothesis that arithmetic features would improve positive recall for the full-residue support reader held on the split. It recovered 528 of 678 positives against zero for the plain tree. Many training and test pairs shared both center and weight at different cutoffs. The experiment isolates a difficult change in a known finite system and measures it exhaustively. Generalization to new populations of centers requires separate evidence.

\subsection{Two controls that accuracy cannot replace}

An exploratory intervention kept the fitted tree fixed and overrode its answer precisely when the known fiber contained both a positive and a negative. A second intervention shifted the weight cyclically within each cutoff before testing the override, and scored the tree on the actual weight. This rotation matched the number of withheld decisions within each cutoff and tag. Both rules withheld 765 state--question pairs. The exact guard blocked every unsupported answer and still left the 105 settled errors. The rotated guard allowed 510 unsupported answers and withheld 510 settled decisions. In terms of public observations, the exact guard found all 45 mixed cases. The rotated guard found 15 of them, because the difference of 510 corresponds to 30 whole fibers of size 17. Merely increasing abstention at a given rate leaves the placement of abstentions unspecified. A paired coverage control makes that placement measurable.

A second control interchanged the genuine tags attached to centers 1 and 2, holding the intended center and the question fixed. Even an exact decoder then turned 48 previously licensed answers into errors across 1,056 center--question pairs. Both tags were individually well formed. The failed premise was their association with the designated object. The later language-model panels supply that association by construction. They test whether a reader uses the possibilities licensed by a supplied record. They give no measurement of retrieval integrity, source authentication or the cost of maintaining a binding in an evolving store.

These pilot results shaped two consequential choices. The scoring key for subsequent panels specifies the action that the record licenses, including a positive obligation to give the appropriate sign when the record settles the question. Both an overconfident and an excessively cautious reader can thereby fail. In addition, each distinct visible observation receives one answer shared across all occurrences, so that a tag representing seventeen possible centers does not produce seventeen nominal reader trials. The changes make the subsequent tests answer a different and more discriminating empirical question. Once all readers see the same compatible states and the same instructions, which readers distinguish agreement across those states from an attractive guess?

\section{The finite experiment as an identification problem}\label{sec:math}
Let $\mathcal S$ be a finite set of histories, $W$ a known deterministic writer, and $q$ a query asked after $W$ has written its record. Denote the truth value in history $s$ by $t_q(s)\in\{0,1\}$. An observer of tag $w$ has access to the inverse image $F_w=\{s:W(s)=w\}$. We call this set a \emph{fiber}. It is the collection of histories the writer has made observationally indistinguishable. All fibers used here are nonempty. An empty fiber would represent an invalid record and would require a distinct rule for a rejected input.

The image $T(w,q)=\{t_q(s):s\in F_w\}$ is the set of answers consistent with the saved evidence. It can be $\{0\}$, $\{1\}$ or $\{0,1\}$. These correspond respectively to N, Y and A. In the experiment A is required in the last case. In some practical systems an agent may decline to answer even when the truth is determined. That system can use the same definition of a supported sign and a different rule for scoring a missed opportunity. Our three-action benchmark rewards the unique maximally informative sound response.

\begin{proposition}[The best coverage available to a sound reader]
Fix the writer, the possible hidden states, the query and the binding between a record and its source. A reader that never makes an unsupported signed assertion can speak definitively only on fibers with $|T(w,q)|=1$. The reader that returns the common sign on each such fiber attains this coverage under every choice of nonnegative observation weights.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
If both signs occur, take a witness of each. The reader receives the same record and query in the two histories. Either definite choice contradicts one witness. If a common sign exists, reporting it satisfies the soundness condition on every witness. Selecting that answer on every singleton image maximizes coverage term by term. A randomized reader with the same universal guarantee also has to put zero probability on both definite actions on a mixed fiber.
\end{proof}

This elementary proposition supplies a control whose failure modes a model comparison otherwise confounds. If even the exact reader must abstain, a new prompt cannot recover universal evidence from that record. If the exact reader can answer but a fitted reader cannot, the available information has survived storage and the observed failure occurs downstream. Improving the writer and improving the reader have distinct counterfactuals. A report that changes both and gives only final truth accuracy cannot generally allocate the improvement between them.

\subsection{A probability law adds a different question}
Let $p$ be the conditional probability of the hidden positive under a specified law on a mixed fiber. A forced binary reader maximizes agreement with the hidden sign by selecting the more frequent sign. Its highest possible conditional accuracy is $\max(p,1-p)$. A universally supported sign exists exactly when the compatible truths coincide. With positive probability on every compatible state these two criteria coincide only at the boundary $p\in\{0,1\}$. A sequence of larger fibers with one exceptional state can have arbitrarily high optimal hidden accuracy and still require an abstention on every observation.

If a decision maker assigns unit cost to a false prediction and cost $c$ to abstaining, a Bayes action may speak whenever $\min(p,1-p)\le c$. This is a rational policy for that law and loss. In our model the question explicitly asks whether \emph{every} compatible integer supports the assertion. A cost threshold has no role in the mathematical target. The experiments nevertheless retain hidden-truth diagnostics under a stated uniform convention so that readers can see the effect of switching losses. For example, a 16-to-1 majority on an ambiguous fiber gives a conditional success probability $16/17$ for N. All seventeen histories yield the same unsupported N in a support audit.

There are two different ways to average this probability. Uniform mass on individual state--query pairs weights a visible observation by its multiplicity. Uniform mass on distinct observations gives a large ambiguous cell one vote. Neither convention is canonical across tasks. The fitted-tree pilot used the former for truth accuracy and exposed how many public observations it represented. The prospective panels use the latter for the primary measurements. Hence a percentage from the pilot cannot be subtracted from a percentage on the model panels to estimate a change in ability.

\subsection{The actual writers and what the reader sees}
In both language-model panels $\mathcal S=\{0,1,\ldots,242\}$. Put $r=S\bmod81$. A public codebook partitions the 81 low residues and assigns an integer tag to each cell. Each prompt prints the tag and its complete decoded cell $R$. Thus
\[
 F_R=\{r+81k:r\in R,\ k=0,1,2\},\qquad |F_R|=3|R|.
\]
The tag carries no implicit ordinal meaning. A row shared by multiple writers asks the same question of the same states. Providing the list evens out the burden of remembering six dictionaries. It also removes an ability that a memory system in production would need, namely finding or decoding the actual stored representation. The experiment starts after that operation.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}p{.20\linewidth}rL@{}}\toprule
Writer & Tags & Rule on the low residue\\\midrule
Three trits &27& Store $r\bmod27$. Each cell is a triple of low residues.\\
Optimized &27& One cell for the 17 formerly empty residues, 11 singletons, seven consecutive triples and eight quadruples.\\
Terminal &65& Store $r$ for $1\le r\le64$ and one shared label otherwise.\\
Split one &66& Separate $65$ from the terminal writer's formerly empty cell.\\
Split eight &66& Separate $65,\ldots,72$ as a group from that cell.\\
Full low residue &81& Each $r$ has its own label. Each label still has three possible lifts in $\mathcal S$.\\\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

Some labels are singletons in low-residue space and three-element sets in hidden-integer space. Which sense of ``one'' is intended determines the answer on the most informative boundary examples. One can preserve all four low ternary digits and still lack the fifth. Conversely, whether the fifth digit matters depends on the query, so counting missing digits also cannot determine the action. A sound benchmark must specify the hidden state space as well as the codebook and observation.

\subsection{What changes at the cutoff}
For a displayed integer $1\le e\le Z$, let $m$ be the smallest power of 3 strictly greater than $Z$. The cutoff is a decision boundary on precision. At $Z=80$ the modulus is 81, whereas at $Z=81$ it is 243. Under the linear rule, the predicate is $S\equiv e\pmod m$. For $m=81$, exactly three lifts agree if $e\bmod81\in R$, and zero otherwise. For $m=243$, exactly one agrees if $e\bmod81\in R$, and zero otherwise. In the latter case every published target lies in a complete system of representatives for $m$, and the three lifts exhaust that low-residue class.

Dividing these counts by $3|R|$ gives the exact conditional truth frequency under uniform lifts. Merely checking whether the numerator vanishes settles all licensed negatives for the linear task. Licensed positives have a stronger condition. In the lower band one needs a singleton $R$ containing $e$; in the upper band positives cannot be licensed. The remaining rows are mixed. These facts explain both the prevalence of N in some panels and the special evidential value of examples that demand Y. A negative-dominated score can mask failure to recognize every positive.

\section{How the experiments were fixed and scored}\label{sec:design}
\subsection{Freezes, amendments and units}
The local protocol began with nine $Z$ values. Cutoff 64 supplied calibration. The eight primary cutoffs were 65, 74, 80, 81, 90, 101, 121 and 162. At each cutoff ten probe cases were projected through six writers. This makes 540 writer occurrences. Exactly equal public observations $(\mathrm{tag},R,Z,e)$ were coalesced, leaving 304 observations. The 33 calibration observations and 271 primary observations jointly filled 38 packets of eight. Of the 271, exactly 13 were licensed Y, 96 licensed N and 162 licensed A. Those numbers describe an intentionally designed set of comparisons. The hidden history that originally suggested a probe is absent from the prompt.

The first freeze included local Llama 3.2, DeepSeek-R1 and GPT-OSS. Four finalized R1 packets produced no usable answers at the 1,536-token limit. On the user's decision, the investigators froze Qwen2.5 as a replacement after inspecting failures. Llama's original receipts carried forward. There were 114 dispatches for the three completed readers and four for the stopped arm. A table containing only the three successful configurations would erase an outcome-dependent selection event. Equally, assigning a semantic answer to the unrun R1 packets would manufacture behavior. We retain the failed arm and the entry date of the replacement.

The hosted study reused the original packet texts, with the local findings already available at registration. Its comparison target was explicit. A hosted configuration needed all 38 packets to return valid answers, all 109 settled signs, and fewer than the 25 unsupported guesses of local GPT-OSS. The two initial hosted arms used GPT-6.2 Sol High and DeepSeek Flash High. A separate supplemental freeze authorized a thinking-off DeepSeek arm following the poor yield of High. This preserves the status of the original experiment while allowing a question about the public usability of another serving mode. The old and supplemental protocols remain separately recoverable.

For transfer the two viable hosted configurations received the new quadratic predicate and an independently hashed order of 304 new rows. Ten fresh cutoffs, 66, 70, 75, 79, 82, 89, 103, 139, 172 and 218, were chosen in advance of dispatch. Different cutoff values alone need not give a fresh mathematical problem. Two can specify the same modulus. The generator also excluded all keys $(\mathrm{tag},R,m,e)$ present on the old panel. The experiment thus rejects exact replay of old effective questions. It retains substantial structure by design, including the six writers, the two moduli, the finite hidden domain and the three-action language.

\subsection{What crossed the interface}
Each call was a fresh context with eight numbered records, common instructions and one request for a JSON object containing eight ordered Y/N/A actions. Readers received the row data and the rule. They lacked the target labels, the actual hidden integer, the quotas, one another's answers and feedback on previous packets. Model-directed tools were unavailable. The local endpoint used a constrained output schema. The hosted arms required the JSON contract in the prompt and validated public output after generation. Cross-environment differences therefore encompass serving and formatting as well as model identity. Within a hosted study the user messages and order matched across its configurations.

The registered maximum was 1,536 output tokens per packet. The hosted transfer run allowed one generation per provider per packet with no repair of malformed replies. A single invalid message deprives eight rows of actions. This matters both statistically and operationally. Rows from one message share a decoding event, a position structure and an all-or-nothing parsing rule. Even with an exhaustive count of these 304 responses one has observed only one rendering and one generation per packet for each configuration. No independence assumption about rows enters the totals.

We distinguish four operational statuses. A valid packet supplies eight alphabet-conforming actions. A finalized invalid packet has a receipt but no usable action under the frozen parser. A reservation without a final receipt leaves actual dispatch uncertain. An unrun packet has neither dispatch evidence nor a final response. The latter two have different accounting consequences. In a budget audit, an uncertain reservation must remain charged against the cap until adjudicated. In the score both represent lack of behavioral evidence. The published receipts distinguish output truncation, which dominated one run, from an otherwise completed prose response that the contract did not accept.

\subsection{Three preregistered linear questions}
H1 asked whether any reader would give a definitive answer on at least one fresh ambiguous observation. All three completed local readers supplied such examples. H2 asked whether at least one could reach 90\% on settled signs and still guess on at least 10\% of ambiguous observations. GPT-OSS reached $109/109$ and $25/162$. High accuracy when the evidence suffices therefore coexisted with failure to abstain when it did not. This conjunction has an interpretable counterexample to a common inference about competence.

H3 was deliberately narrower. The specified full-residue record 80 with query 80 changes from Y at $Z=80$ to A at $Z=81$. The failure registered in advance was Y followed by Y. Qwen produced it. Llama said N twice, a different pair of failures; GPT-OSS switched as required. Llama missed both endpoints. Qwen identified the first and retained its Y when the later record required A. Other probes, additional boundary pairs and packet-position counts carry descriptive rather than prospective status unless they were part of the original freeze.

\subsection{Scores that expose the cost of missing output}
Write $n_A$ for scheduled ambiguous rows, $g$ for observed signed guesses, $a$ for explicit A and $u_A$ for missing valid actions. Then $n_A=g+a+u_A$. A usefully precise summary comprises all three quantities
\[
    \frac{g}{g+a},\qquad \frac{a}{n_A},\qquad
       \left[\frac{g}{n_A},\frac{g+u_A}{n_A}\right].
\]
The first fraction conditions on a usable public response and is undefined if there are none. The second gives the proportion of scheduled ambiguous questions on which the reader actually supplied the licensed response. The interval gives the range of possible guess rates if the missing answers are assigned arbitrary potential actions. It is a logical sensitivity calculation. It has no sampling interpretation and imputes nothing to the actual transcript. Because missingness occurs by packet, rowwise bounds need not describe some future run with the same parser. Their endpoints are attainable by completing all missing rows as A or all as definite.

On the settled set use $s$ for the number of observed licensed signs, $w$ for wrong signs, $v$ for unnecessary abstentions and $u_D$ for missing actions. Then $n_D=s+w+v+u_D$. The joint criterion demands both high $s/n_D$ and low $g/n_A$, supplemented by the explicit-abstention and availability counts. Always abstaining gets $g=0$ without resolving any settled question. Always answering a sign can attain all settled signs while guessing on every ambiguous case. A single scalar can rank those two behaviors only by pricing their failures or fixing their relative frequency in a population.

\section{What the linear panel reveals about abstention}\label{sec:linear}
As Figure~\ref{fig:linear} shows, the two columns that most change the interpretation are the settled positives and the explicit abstentions. A reader can score highly on settled negatives by saying N almost everywhere. It can produce zero observed guesses by returning no public answer. In Table~\ref{tab:linear} these behaviors lead to very different rows. Counts in the two signed columns are successful answers of that sign. An A is counted in the fifth column only when the key licenses A.

\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrrrr@{}}\toprule
Reader & Y & N & Guesses & Explicit A & Invalid & Unrun\\\midrule
Llama 3.2 &0&85&152&10&0&0\\
Qwen2.5 &6&18&84&78&0&0\\
GPT-OSS &13&96&25&137&0&0\\
Local DeepSeek-R1 &0&0&0&0&26&245\\\midrule
Sol High &13&96&0&162&0&0\\
Flash High &0&8&0&6&257&0\\
Flash thinking off &11&65&89&69&7&0\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Three local readers and a stopped arm}
Llama offered the clearest example of a high negative hit count with a one-sided policy. All thirteen settled positives went unrecognized, while 85 settled negatives received a licensed N. Of 152 unsupported assertions, 150 were N. Under uniform hidden integers within each observation, the expected hidden-truth agreement of the guesses is about 82.84\%. The same decisions yield zero defensible assertions on those 152 observations. Both calculations describe the transcript. They answer different questions about a mechanism facing lossy storage.

Qwen's errors spanned a different part of the action space. It acknowledged 78 of the ambiguous records and guessed on 84. In 54 of these guesses it chose Y. It signed only 24 of 109 settled records successfully. Of the other 85, 27 received the opposite sign and 58 an unnecessary abstention. A universal preference for withholding cannot account for this combination. GPT-OSS signed all 109 settled cases successfully yet guessed on 25 ambiguous ones. The split is arithmetically informative. It guessed on none of the 36 ambiguous observations below the precision boundary and on 25 of 126 above it. Fifteen of those guesses occurred among 34 upper-band ambiguous singleton records. A singleton low residue can be mistaken for a uniquely identified integer precisely where the fifth ternary digit can matter. The pattern suggests this hypothesis. The public responses do not reveal the internal procedure.

The smaller local DeepSeek-R1 received four eight-row packets. All reached the token ceiling without a usable public answer. Its 32 invalid row slots comprised 26 primary and six calibration questions. The remaining 272 row slots were never sent, including 245 primary. There is no observed guess in this arm and no interpretable behavioral comparison with any completed reader. Its failure belongs in the paper because the choice of Qwen as a replacement was informed by these outcomes and because real systems must budget tokens for public decisions. The label DeepSeek appears again in the hosted experiments on a different model. Combining the local R1 prefix and hosted Flash receipts would create a configuration that no one ran.

On calibration, the completed local readers correctly signed 23, 9 and 26 of 26 settled observations, respectively, in Llama, Qwen and GPT-OSS order. Their unsupported guesses were 6, 2 and 0 on seven ambiguous observations. The calibration rows were retained in packets and are included in the 304-row total, while the preregistered linear performance criteria use only the 271 primary rows. A reader who wishes to reconstruct an overall score can add the counts from each disjoint subset. Pooling them by default would obscure a difference between a reference cutoff already analyzed by the investigators and cutoffs chosen for the prospective reader test.

\subsection{A hosted success and a comparison limited by output}
Sol High returned 38 valid packets. It selected the licensed sign for all 109 primary settled records and explicitly abstained on all 162 primary ambiguous ones. On calibration it also returned all 26 licensed signs and seven required abstentions. The observed total is therefore 304/304. This exceeded the registered hosted target. The result is stronger than agreement with a default sign or with the forced-majority rule, both of which make a definitive assertion on every ambiguous question. It leaves open how the system recognized the support of a fresh display.

DeepSeek Flash High returned two valid packets. The remaining 36 hit the token ceiling and yielded no usable public action. On the 14 usable primary records, its six ambiguous answers were all A and its eight settled answers were all licensed negatives. None of the 13 licensed positives appeared in that jointly observable portion of the High run. Of the 162 primary ambiguous rows, 156 lacked a usable action. Any imputed full-panel guess count from zero through 156 is compatible with that absence of evidence. Publishing a zero-percent guess rate without the number of missing observations would make this a misleading competitor for Sol's complete result.

The thinking-off supplement produced 37 valid packets, with one prose reply rejected by the frozen parser. Four ambiguous rows and three settled negatives were missing in the primary sample. Flash off guessed on 89/158 usable ambiguous rows, split 34 yes and 55 no. It supplied 76/106 licensed signs on usable settled questions, including 11/13 positives. Relative to the full scheduled set its positive contribution was 69/162 explicit abstentions and 76/109 licensed signs. Its possible guess count with arbitrary completions spans 89 to 93. Its already observed 89 guesses alone exceed the target of fewer than 25, even though the original complete-coverage comparison protocol records the overall verdict as unassessable. A failed necessary condition and an unscorable complete run are logically compatible.

Only 14 primary rows had valid public answers in \emph{both} hosted Flash modes. High chose the licensed action on all 14. Flash off matched it on two of six ambiguous rows and six of eight settled negatives. Thus turning thinking off produced many more scoreable outputs under this ceiling, but it did not win the comparison of licensed decisions within the overlap. Nor can High's 14 matched actions be extrapolated to its 257 missing primary rows. The stopping mechanism selected which questions would be available for comparison in a way this experiment cannot characterize.

\begin{figure}[!htb]\centering
\bandfigure{.97\linewidth}{linear}
\caption{Scheduled and observed linear outcomes. Counts belong to the same primary set. The ranges for incomplete arms show the effect of assigning potential actions to missing rows.}\label{fig:linear}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Why the controls are part of the result}
The exact support oracle simultaneously achieves 109/109 settled signs and 162/162 licensed abstentions. A forced-majority oracle reaches 109/109 settled signs as well, yet guesses on all 162 ambiguous records. Always saying N earns 96/109 settled signs while missing all 13 positives and guessing on the entire ambiguous set. Always abstaining eliminates every unsupported signed answer at the price of zero settled signs. These four comparators span behaviors that a conventional accuracy-only report could mistake for successful reading. In particular, a strong settled score gives no logical upper bound on the guess count unless some additional behavioral assumption links the two.

The same principle gives a decision-oriented reading of the model results. If a user values a rare licensed positive, Llama's 85 licensed negatives do little to compensate for missing all thirteen primary positives. If a user demands an answer to every identifiable question, Qwen's large number of unnecessary abstentions is consequential even though it abstains more appropriately on ambiguous records than Llama does. The experiment supplies the loss components so a reader can price these trade-offs openly. It does not supply a population distribution or a marginal utility of an assertion for some unrelated task.

\section{Why squaring is a discriminating intervention}\label{sec:square}
Changing the predicate to $S^2\equiv e\pmod m$ is modest in the prompt but substantial at the information boundary. On a linear upper-band singleton, a positive match can never be universal. After squaring it can be. Moreover, the same three hidden lifts can yield three different answers to the remainder question or one repeated remainder. Merely memorizing the upper-band policy for the previous task now creates counterexamples. The exact form of the change can be derived before consulting any model.

\begin{lemma}[Quadratic image of a low-residue class]\label{lem:lift}
Let $0\le r<81$ and $k\in\{0,1,2\}$. At modulus 81 all three squares $(r+81k)^2$ have remainder $r^2\bmod81$. At modulus 243 they have remainders
\[
 r^2,\quad r^2+162r,\quad r^2+324r\pmod{243}.
\]
If $3$ divides $r$, these three remainders coincide. Otherwise they are the three lifts modulo 243 of the low square $r^2\bmod81$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Expanding produces a final term $81^2k^2$ divisible by 243. The step between consecutive remainders is $81\cdot 2r$. In the group of multiples of 81 modulo 243 that step is zero when $3\mid r$. Otherwise multiplication by $2r$ permutes the three residues modulo 3. Reducing all remainders modulo 81 proves the final assertion.
\end{proof}

Let $h_r$ denote the number of the three lifts whose square matches the query. At the lower modulus $h_r$ is 3 or 0 according as $r^2\equiv e\pmod{81}$ or otherwise. At modulus 243 a multiple of three contributes 3 or 0 according to the full congruence $r^2\equiv e\pmod{243}$. A unit contributes 1 if its square matches modulo 81 and 0 otherwise. Thus $H=\sum_{r\in R}h_r$ is the exact number of satisfying histories. Return N when $H=0$, Y when $H=3|R|$, and A at every intermediate value. The frozen generator enumerates the candidates; a separate checker implements the algebra and verifies the published labels against the prompts. Agreement between methods exposes mistakes such as dropping a lift or using a non-strict cutoff.

\subsection{Where uncertainty can enter}
The average $H/(3|R|)$ can be intermediate for two logically different reasons. Distinct listed low residues may have different unanimous answers. Alternatively, lifts of a single listed residue may disagree. Some rows combine split and uniform triples. These sources have different remedies. Learning the exact low residue would settle the first kind of ambiguity. It can leave the second unresolved. Conversely, learning the fifth ternary digit while still lacking the low residue does not automatically settle a row where different low residues give opposing unanimous answers.

An existential statement suffices for one side of several tempting shortcuts. A square match for one $s$ disproves a universally justified N and leaves two candidate actions, Y and A. One failed match disproves a universally justified Y and leaves N and A. To certify A one needs two witnesses of opposite signs. To certify Y or N by enumeration one must cover the entire fiber, or give an algebraic proof that all members share the licensed sign. The support oracle distinguishes these obligations. Its determinate answer can be much cheaper to verify algebraically than a long census suggests.

\subsection{A derivation that suggests an independent future test}
The square is one polynomial for which the image of a low-residue class can be read from a derivative. More generally let $f\in\mathbb Z[X]$. For $s=r+81k$, binomial expansion gives
\[
 f(s)\equiv f(r)+81k f'(r)\pmod{243}.
\]
Every term of order two or higher in $81k$ is divisible by 243. Hence each low-residue class has one image modulo 243 if $f'(r)\equiv0\pmod3$, and three otherwise. This is a mathematical deduction for a one-digit lift. It is not an observed transfer result for other predicates. It identifies precisely which arithmetic property our change of rule introduced and exposes a way to intervene on it in a subsequent study.

For example $f(X)=X^2+X$ yields $f'(r)=2r+1$, so the flat class is $r\equiv1\pmod3$, in place of $r\equiv0\pmod3$ for the square. A reader that learned only the square-specific cue that multiples of three have unanimous lifts would now encounter explicit counterexamples. At $r=1$, all three linear-plus-square values agree with 2 modulo 243. At $r=0$ their images are $0,81,162$ modulo 243. The first example can use the allowed query $e=2$. For the second use $e=81$ at a cutoff at least 81 so that the query is in range. The former licenses Y and the latter A. The variant holds the storage model fixed while changing which residue class suppresses lift variation. No generations for this variant enter the present study.

\subsection{What changed in the sampling frame}
Six codebooks yield 127 distinct public tag/cell pairs. For ten selected cutoffs and each legal $e$ there are 138,811 raw public tuples. Different cutoffs in one modulus band can describe the same effective query. The generator collapsed these tuples to $(\mathrm{tag},R,m,e)$ and excluded every prior-panel key. This left 37,554 eligible effective queries. Within each modulus and transition from the old to new licensed action it filled fixed quotas, balancing selected structural classes and using deterministic hash ranks. Six anchor records were prespecified. The choice of one legal cutoff for each key and a separate hash ordering finalized the 38 packets.

\begin{table}[htbp]\centering\small
\caption{Selection changes the task faced by a constant reader. Entries are counts of rows or keys according to the unit in each column.}\label{tab:base}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrr@{}}\toprule
Property & Raw cutoff tuples & Eligible keys & Panel\\\midrule
Number of items &138,811&37,554&304\\
Old rule matches new &132,734&36,012&64\\
Old rule conflicts with new &6,077&1,542&240\\
New action N &135,935&36,809&139\\
New action Y &400&102&65\\
New action A &2,476&643&100\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Figure~\ref{fig:enrich} shows how the conflict rate changes from the census to the administered panel. In the eligible pool an exact old-rule reader would score 95.89\%, and always saying N would score 98.02\%. On the chosen panel their agreement with the new key drops to 21.05\% and 45.72\%, respectively. A random sample of this particular pool could reward both strategies handsomely while offering relatively little power to discriminate between them. The quota design made the question harder in a specified direction and made its result easier to interpret. Its pass rate estimates none of these strategies' prevalence in ordinary memory use. For the measured models, there are no outcomes on the 37,250 unqueried eligible keys.

\begin{figure}[!htb]\centering
\bandfigure{.97\linewidth}{enrich}
\caption{Conflict enrichment changes the distribution faced by each reader. Raw cutoff tuples count different printed cutoff values separately. Eligible keys collapse equal moduli and exclude old questions. The administered panel deliberately favors conflicts.}\label{fig:enrich}
\end{figure}

Not every transition between Y, N and A is reachable. The enumeration found zero Y-to-A and A-to-Y items in this census. One explanation for the first zero can be derived without looking at codebooks. Linear Y requires a low-band singleton whose residue equals the query. In that band its three square values agree. The square action for such a row may be Y or N, never A. The second zero admits a short proof for this design. Linear A requires a positive linear witness, so the candidate $e$ itself is compatible in the upper band. In the lower band its low residue is listed and $e$ is likewise compatible. Quadratic Y then implies $e^2\equiv e\pmod m$. Since $e$ and $e-1$ are coprime and $m$ is a prime power with $1\le e<m$, we must have $e=1$. At modulus 243 the lifts of 1 have distinct squares. At modulus 81 linear A requires more than one low residue, and the only such cell containing 1 is $\{1,28,55\}$. But $28^2\equiv55\pmod{81}$. Each case contradicts quadratic Y. One should not convert an empty transition cell into a theorem about all possible writers and queries. For instance, in a different codebook a low-band cell $\{1,80\}$ with $e=1$ admits mixed linear truths and unanimous square truth.


\section{Transfer scores and the evidence for an old-rule explanation}\label{sec:transfer}
Both hosted configurations provided 38 valid packets on the square rule. Missing outputs therefore contribute nothing to the difference between the two observed scores. Sol returned the licensed action on 65/65 settled positives, 139/139 settled negatives and 100/100 ambiguous records. Flash off answered 8/65 positives and 77/139 negatives with the licensed sign. It abstained explicitly on 32/100 ambiguous records. Its 187 mismatches comprise 66 wrong signs on settled records, 53 unnecessary abstentions on settled records and 68 unsupported signs on ambiguous records. Table~\ref{tab:squareconf} gives the underlying counts. An intervention that repairs one kind of error could leave the other two untouched.

\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrrrr@{}}\toprule
&\multicolumn{3}{c}{Sol High}&\multicolumn{3}{c}{Flash thinking off}\\
\cmidrule(lr){2-4}\cmidrule(l){5-7}
Required&Y&N&A&Y&N&A\\\midrule
