\subsection{What a conflict row can establish}
On a conflict the old and new prescribed actions are different. Figure~\ref{fig:conflict} separates the three valid responses. A model can match the quadratic rule, match the linear rule or choose the remaining action. Sol's counts are 240, zero and zero. Flash off's are 79, 140 and 21. On 64 agreement controls the two models scored 64 and 38. Sol therefore satisfies the registered all-row transfer criterion; each of Flash off's 187 disagreements is sufficient to falsify that same claim for its run. All of these statements could be adjudicated without seeing any model's private reasoning.

A uniform old-rule policy would score 64 on the panel. Flash off scored 117. The 79 new-rule conflict decisions already exclude unvarying application of the old predicate. Whether the 140 old-action matches reveal a partial inclination to apply it is harder. On this particular conflict set there are 64 N-to-Y and 80 N-to-A rows. The simple policy of always answering N therefore matches the \emph{old} action on 144/240 conflicts. A count near 140 can readily be obtained with no access to the historical predicate. This is an identification problem for the proposed explanation, separate from whether the classification of old-action outputs is accurate.

Compare actual decisions with constant N one row at a time. Both are licensed on 77 records. Only Flash is licensed on 40, namely eight settled positives and 32 required abstentions. Only constant N is licensed on 62, namely the settled negatives where Flash answered otherwise. Neither is licensed on 125. The overall difference $40-62=-22$ explains the apparent paradox that a reader with two abilities a constant negative lacks can score below it. The relative value of detecting positives, withholding on ambiguous cases and correctly excluding a possibility determines how one should assess this difference for a downstream purpose.

In a post hoc check, group the 240 conflicts by the new action, modulus and whether the printed list has one or several low residues. In each of the eleven occupied groups the old action takes only one value. For example, at modulus 81 on square-negative conflicts, all 32 old-Y cases have singleton lists, and all 32 old-A cases have multiple low residues. The model produced 6 Y, 20 N and 6 A in the former group and 2 Y, 9 N and 21 A in the latter. Conditional on these readily visible properties and the new target, the experiment has no within-group variation in the old label with which to estimate its independent behavioral effect. This result specifies an experimental limitation much more tightly than saying a model is opaque.

\begin{figure}[!htb]\centering
\bandfigure{.98\linewidth}{conflict}
\caption{Decisions by the transition between the two mathematical targets. A historical target is the action licensed by the linear rule on the new record. It is computed without showing that record to a model under the old instructions.}\label{fig:conflict}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Public records, effective questions and conflicting answers}
The transfer study removes a source of repetition in the linear benchmark. A visible linear row has the form $(t,R,Z,e)$, where $t$ is the tag. For fixed $t,R,e$ changing $Z$ without changing the effective modulus leaves the finite truth test invariant. We grouped the archived rows by $(t,R,m,e)$ after the runs. The 304 visible linear rows correspond to 170 distinct effective keys, and the 271 primary rows to 153. Within the primary set, 47 keys occur at least twice, covering 165 displayed observations. The other 106 keys are displayed once. This grouping is an equivalence with respect to the prescribed mathematics. Different wordings or packet neighbors can still affect a decoder.

For each repeated key we asked whether its valid returned actions included at least two distinct symbols. Among the fully observed local readers the counts were 18/47 for Llama, 31/47 for Qwen and 10/47 for GPT-OSS. Sol High changed on none of the 47. Flash off produced conflicting actions on 39 of the 47 keys; the invalid packet means that some assigned repeated rows have unknown actions. For Flash High only one repeated key had two valid primary actions, with no change on that key. The incomplete arm supplies almost no evidence about the stability of its behavior on equivalent questions.

The case $R=\{0\}, e=81$ is particularly revealing. Across the five cutoffs 81, 90, 101, 121 and 162 the candidates are always $0,81,162$, of which only 81 matches. GPT-OSS's sequence in increasing-cutoff order was A, Y, A, N, N. Neither the compatible integers nor the modulus changed. One cannot associate this sequence with cutoff alone because packet position, neighboring questions and generation context could also vary. One can say that no deterministic function of $(t,R,m,e)$ alone reproduces this realized response sequence. The latter is a falsifiable behavioral statement considerably stronger than citation of a single failed example.

The same counting distinction qualifies the combined Sol headline. Across both panels it supplied 608 licensed actions in 608 visible row presentations. There are 170 distinct effective keys on the linear panel and 304 on the square panel, with no overlap. This gives 474 effective keys in the union. The two predicates belong to one arithmetic family and the two question sets reuse the same writers. Neither 608 nor 474 is a sample size for independent draws from arbitrary reasoning problems. Both numbers convey something important. Repeated renderings test a degree of invariance, whereas fresh keys increase the variety of mathematics tested.




\section{Reading individual decisions}
\label{sec:reader-decisions}

A total score tells us how often a reader chose the licensed action. To determine which distinctions the score actually tests, we can hold a record fixed and vary the precision of the question, compare two predicates on the same admissible hidden integers, or keep the question fixed and enlarge the record's set of possibilities. The archived rows permit all three comparisons. The first two studies use the same linear prompts. In the transfer study each row has two computed reference answers, one linear and one quadratic, and the model saw only the quadratic question. A reader's “old action” on a transfer row therefore means its output coincides with a counterfactual reference label. There is no earlier model answer to that same row.

\subsection{A linear boundary with two nearly identical switches}

Take the public record $R=\{80\}$ with query $e=80$. The compatible integers are $80,161,242$. At cutoff $80$ the modulus is $81$, so every compatible integer satisfies the question. Increasing the cutoff to $81$ changes the modulus to $243$. Now $80$ supplies a yes witness, while $161$ and $242$ are witnesses for the negative sign. A licensed pair of decisions has to change from Y to A. The frozen row identifiers are \texttt{86f6ad9c7e05e26425} and \texttt{861d5bc3cd85e1bf47}. The first query is fully settled even though the reader cannot determine the integer. The second becomes ambiguous without losing a single bit of the stored record. The measurement simply asks for a distinction that the record never made.

On this pair, local Qwen printed Y twice. Local Llama printed N twice. GPT-OSS and hosted Sol each printed Y followed by A. Hosted DeepSeek with thinking off printed Y followed by N. Under a uniform draw from the three compatible integers, N predicts the hidden truth twice as often as Y at the upper cutoff. Nevertheless the assertion N has the public counterexample $80$. Calling the response a successful detection of a precision change would conceal the actual issue. A change in the answer and a change to the licensed answer are different observable events.

The same calculation works for $R=\{65\}$ with $e=65$ and lifts $65,146,227$. Here the archived pair is \texttt{0bf126c7db07e1f9b7} and \texttt{1ff0884e0958403a72}. GPT-OSS kept Y at both cutoffs. DeepSeek with thinking off switched from Y to A. Sol again followed the licensed pair. The two similar switches reverse the ordering one might assign to GPT-OSS and DeepSeek if one picked a favorite example. The residue-80 comparison was registered in advance in the local study. The residue-65 comparison was identified by subsequently matching the local panel, and both examples were already known when the hosted study began. Their evidential roles differ even though their algebra is identical.

There were 14 exact matches between the two boundary cutoffs on the triple consisting of numerical tag, complete public residue list, and query. Of these, two needed Y to change to A, five needed N at both cutoffs, and seven needed A at both. GPT-OSS matched both ends on 13 pairs, Llama on five, and Qwen on one. Sol matched both ends on all 14. DeepSeek with thinking off matched both on eight, comprising one switch, four stable negatives, and three stable abstentions. A rule that recycled the earlier licensed answer would succeed on 12 of the 14 pairs. A rule that solved the earlier query and always abstained at the later cutoff would succeed on nine, including both switches. The stable pairs distinguish a change justified by particular lifts from indiscriminate caution at the boundary. All complete pairs remain within a single selected benchmark. The two observations in a pair may have occupied different positions among different neighboring questions.

\subsection{How an answer position can imitate selective judgment}

Among the 162 ambiguous primary linear rows, Llama abstained on ten. Nine of those ten appeared in position two. Viewed from the other direction, it abstained on nine of the 21 ambiguous rows in that position and on one of the 141 ambiguous rows elsewhere. An account based only on successful abstentions might give the impression that the second position contained especially transparent uncertainty. A negative control within the same experiment gives reason for caution. Ten of the 14 settled negative rows in position two also elicited an abstention. Only one of the other 82 settled negative rows did so. Thus the tendency appeared both where abstention was warranted and where abstention withheld an answer the record had settled.

This is an exploratory association discovered after inspection. Each row occupied one position and had one set of neighbors. Rearranging which observations went in position two could alter its apparent effect even if a model responded identically whenever it saw a given question. Conversely a real position effect could have contributed to the observed pattern. The data contain no counterfactual placement for the same question. An appropriate follow-up would rotate each intact packet through all eight positions in fresh contexts and compare each row with itself. A second manipulation could then vary neighbors while holding position fixed. These are different tests because rotating a packet holds its membership fixed and changes which members come first.

\subsection{The agreement controls that were exhaustively covered}
The 64 agreement controls constrain a different class of explanation. Sol matched all 64 and DeepSeek matched 38. The latter count comprises one of one shared Y, 31 of 43 shared N, and six of 20 shared A. The score of 38 cannot be credited to discrimination between the formulas, because either formula licenses the same action in each control. Conversely the 26 misses on controls show that predicate switching alone is insufficient to account for the shortfall. There is an unusually clear finite boundary on one control class. After excluding the historical semantic keys, the entire eligible generator produced only six shared-A keys at modulus 81 and fourteen at modulus 243. The transfer panel contains every one of those twenty keys. The sole eligible shared-Y key is also present, with $R=\{1\}$, $e=1$, $Z=66$ (\texttt{ff4b16bd9dd158dbde}). This is complete key coverage for those two cells. Each key still had a single displayed cutoff, position, and packet realization. The 43 shared-N controls represent only a selection from the much larger pool of shared negatives.

\subsection{Six specified anchors, three different controlled changes}

At $R=\{2\}$, $e=4$, the allowed integers are $2,83,164$. Their squares are all congruent to $4$ modulo $81$. Modulo $243$, their square residues in the same order are $4,85,166$. Thus the lower query licenses Y and the upper query licenses A. The prior linear rule gives N in both cases. DeepSeek printed N at the lower cutoff (\texttt{22b43d12faca657eea}) and Y at the upper (\texttt{8e4fa66cb1361e004f}). Its first return matches the old rule, while the second follows neither rule. Sol printed Y then A. Replacing an outdated negative at the upper cutoff with the affirmative square of the visible representative still neglects the two additional lifts.

Now take $R=\{3\}$ and $e=9$. The possible integers are $3,84,165$. Their squares are all $9$ modulo $243$, hence also $9$ modulo $81$. Both cutoffs license Y and both readers printed Y on both observations (\texttt{a42531e360b5dcf76f} and \texttt{0313a36326343fc32b}). These two cutoff rows shared packet 5, in positions three and eight. The upper $R=\{2\}$ question was in position two of that same packet. DeepSeek therefore returned the licensed stable affirmative and missed a nearby split in the same response array. These entries give a within-packet comparison even though the neighboring rows and positions can still influence individual answers.

The two anchors at $Z=82$ with $e=81$ keep the question and tag fixed while enlarging the public residue list from $\{9\}$ to $\{9,36,63\}$. Every compatible integer in the expanded record has the form $9+27j$. Squaring gives $81+486j+729j^2$, which is $81$ modulo $243$ for every integer $j$. All nine lifts therefore give yes, as do the three compatible with the smaller record. Sol printed Y on both rows. DeepSeek gave N for the singleton (\texttt{57a19b293a80b8c734}) and A for its enlargement (\texttt{d3bf062a3b6231e995}). The first answer reverses a settled sign. The second forgoes a universally valid sign. A larger set of possible hidden integers can add several different witnesses for exactly the same signed claim.

\subsection{Following one question through both square moduli}

We made an additional retrospective match across the two modulus bands of the quadratic panel. The matching key retains the tag, complete residue list, and $e$. Only the displayed cutoff and its modulus change in the mathematical question. Since there is one observation per semantic key in either band, every cross-band match has precisely two ends. Twenty-one such pairs exist. Their 42 observations are already included in the 304-row totals. They measure how much of each comparison survives when both outcomes must be licensed.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
Required low/high actions & Pairs & Sol, both licensed & DeepSeek off, both licensed\\
\midrule
Y then A & 6 & 6 & 1\\
Y then Y & 5 & 5 & 1\\
N then N & 6 & 6 & 1\\
A then A & 4 & 4 & 0\\
\midrule
All & 21 & 21 & 3\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The one switch on which both readers followed the change uses $R=\{12,39,66\}$ and $e=63$. At cutoff $79$ every lift satisfies the square query (\texttt{a37fd4234d25e9e427}). At cutoff $89$, the square residues for representatives $12,39,66$ are respectively $144,63,225$ modulo $243$. Each representative is a multiple of three and each of its three lifts preserves its square residue. Now exactly three of the nine integers work, and the public row licenses A (\texttt{dc1e39be02214e4540}). The loss of unanimity here occurs entirely between distinct low residues. In the $R=\{2\}$ example it occurs entirely within a single low residue. Both printed Y-to-A sequences have the same logical meaning when licensed. The arithmetic obstruction can occupy a different level of the record.

Stable pairs impose a complementary obligation. A change in numerical precision can leave every relevant square unchanged, leave every square outside the query, or preserve witnesses on both sides. Those outcomes require Y/Y, N/N, and A/A respectively. An always-change rule would miss all fifteen stable pairs. Reusing the earlier oracle answer would pass those fifteen and miss the six switches. The pair selection here follows a post-run matching operation. Matching removes differences in the public record and query. Packet membership, position, and cutoff values within each modulus band remain uncontrolled.

\subsection{What counts as a guessing error}

Each of the 68 unsupported square answers from DeepSeek has two directly inspectable hidden witnesses. For example, in packet 1, position eight (\texttt{4c56f2666249afad54}), the public set is $\{12,13,14\}$ and the target is $63$ modulo $81$. The model answered N. But $12^2\equiv63$ and $13^2\equiv7\pmod{81}$, with both integers admissible. In packet 2, position one (\texttt{5226ce330536525132}), the visible set runs from $65$ to $72$ and the target is $144$ modulo $243$. The model answered Y. Yet $69^2\equiv144$ while $65^2\equiv94\pmod{243}$. These examples pick opposite guess directions in packet order. Either can be checked without attributing a belief, probability, or hidden strategy to the model.

In all 100 ambiguous square records in this panel there are strictly more falsifying integers than satisfying ones. This finite fact follows by counting compatible lifts. It does not impose a probability law. If one adds the assumption that all compatible integers in each record have equal probability, 58 of the 68 unsupported guesses choose the more likely sign. Giving equal weight to each guessed observation, their mean probability of matching the unobserved hidden truth is $3201/4624$, approximately $69.2\%$. One obtains this number by summing the compatible proportion for the sign actually printed on each guessed row and dividing by 68. It is an expectation over an explicitly chosen distribution. The experiment drew no hidden integer to grade these guesses, and every one of the 68 has a concrete counterexample to its assertion.

The location of those counterexamples is informative. On 58 ambiguous records each individual low residue is internally uniform, and distinct low residues give opposite truths. DeepSeek guessed on 43, comprising 35 N and eight Y. On 32 records some individual residues split while others are uniform. It guessed N on 16 and abstained on 16. On ten records every low residue splits. All ten in this panel are singletons, and the reader guessed on nine, seven N and two Y. Sol abstained on every observation in all three classes. The last denominator matters. Here the properties “one residue” and “every residue splits” coincide on the ambiguous records. Consequently the observed nine out of ten cannot establish which feature makes the example difficult. One feasible control would supply the new public list $R=\{2,79\}$ at modulus $243$ and ask for $e=4$. Each of its two low residues splits. The lifts $2$ and $241$ satisfy the query while $83$ and $79$ fail it. Such a case would allow a comparison inside the all-split class across two list sizes. A singleton with an internally uniform triple cannot be ambiguous, so a complete factorial crossing of these properties is mathematically impossible.

The within-packet unit also changes what one can conclude. Across the 38 complete square responses, DeepSeek licensed zero to six actions per eight-question packet. Twelve packets contributed exactly three and one packet contributed zero. In that zero-credit packet, number 15, the required sequence was A, A, N, A, N, A, N, A. The returned sequence was N, N, A, N, A, N, A, N. All five ambiguous questions drew a definite negative and all three settled negatives drew abstention. The response is usable as a complete ordered array. Its pattern of errors survives even if one gives full credit for reply format. It also illustrates why an eight-row invalid response and eight usable but poorly aligned answers warrant distinct records in the analysis. In the first case one does not know what individual actions were chosen. In the second there is sufficient public evidence to audit every action independently.

\section{What the experiments distinguish}\label{sec:interpret}
\subsection{Three accounts with different observable consequences}
One account of the original perfect linear score was that a system could read a witness record sufficiently well to determine whether the stated predicate holds on its entire fiber. This account permits enumeration, a modular shortcut, or some combination. It predicts the observed success on both changed-rule conflicts and stable controls if the required operations continue to fit within the token and task constraints. The results are consistent with that level of behavioral competence for Sol. They do not discriminate enumeration from efficient arithmetic. This distinction is especially important because the closed-form square rule in Lemma~\ref{lem:lift} can reproduce every answer without enumerating the three lifts one by one.

A second account was exact reuse of the old linear action whenever confronted with this data format. Its prediction was 64/304 on the transfer panel and zero on conflicts. Both models violate that prediction. Sol contradicted the old action on all 240 conflicts. Flash off contradicted it on 100 conflicts, comprising 79 new-rule matches and 21 third actions. Its 26 misses on agreement controls raise its total disagreements with the old policy to 126 of 304. The experiment can thus rule out exact reuse for either recorded transcript. A claim that a model sometimes relied on some component of the old policy remains underspecified until it predicts where and how often. The constant-N comparison and the empty within-stratum contrasts identify why a raw count of matches cannot supply that theory.

A third account was a policy that equates loss of the upper ternary digit with mandatory abstention at the higher cutoff. The square rule permits upper-band unanimous positives and negatives. The $r=9$ anchor provides a direct counterexample to the policy and an observed response for both configurations. A distinct possible shortcut is checking just the smallest representative. It succeeds at some anchors and fails at $r=2$ where an admissible larger representative changes the truth. The importance of these cases is their disagreement with specified, testable policies. Neither a successful answer nor an error on one of them identifies all the computations that led to it.

Many alternative procedures produce the same finite outputs. A table that stores every displayed input and its recorded answer would do so exactly. A program implementing the algebra would produce the same results as Sol on these panels while making predictions for infinitely many other rendered queries. The experiment distinguishes these procedures only where their outcomes diverge. Freshness of the quadratic panel relative to the local run is genuine evidence against a simple lookup of those earlier keys. It cannot independently rule out general familiarity with modular arithmetic, exposure during pretraining, or a response strategy that would fail on another rule. The empirical advance is that a specific family of competing predictions has been tested under known controls.

\subsection{Where comparison across studies is justified}
The same linear panel supports row-for-row comparison across the three completed local readers and the hosted arms. Matched observations remove variation due to which particular items were shown. When one configuration returns little usable output, comparison on matched usable observations can still suffer selection by the generation process. The R1 stop and the Flash High ceiling demonstrate two different forms of this problem. No amount of arithmetic reconstruction of the oracle produces a missing model decision. A missingness bound is an honest description of what the available evidence can settle.

The transfer study answers a prospective question motivated by an earlier success. It changes the function from linear to quadratic and changes the panel, its cutoff values, class frequencies and mixture of conflicts. These changes are appropriate for challenging the old rule. They prevent a subtraction of the two panels' overall accuracies from identifying a causal difficulty penalty for squaring. A paired experiment on the same new records with both instructions would estimate a different contrast. The old-action columns in the existing transfer ledger are exact mathematical counterfactuals for the \emph{oracle}. There were no paired old-instruction model outputs for those same keys.

The exact score and the operational score also have different units of replication. All eligible states can be enumerated, so a finite query's target has no sampling uncertainty under the definitions. A frozen set of public model responses likewise yields exact counts. Repeating the service calls might produce different outputs, and selecting a new query family might change the task substantially. Neither uncertainty is captured by pretending the 608 presentations are independent draws. There is no replication for the distribution over inference runs at fixed packet inputs. There are two related predicates, which is too narrow a basis for a population estimate over predicates. The honest uncertainty ledger consists of explicit observational equivalences, missingness intervals and bounded scope, not a binomial interval for an unspecified population.

\subsection{How much of the eligible pool can a perfect score certify?}
Consider even the deliberately narrow target of performance on the 37,554 eligible quadratic keys. To define such a target, fix in advance one rendering and context for each additional key, together with the observed response for each measured key. Without an assumption relating tested and untested keys, the maximum number of additional successes is 37,250 and the minimum is zero. For Sol this gives the identification interval $[304,37{,}554]$ for the total number of licensed actions. For Flash off it gives $[117,37{,}367]$. These intervals describe arbitrary completions of the unobserved finite table. They are intentionally wide. Restricting the target to a census of a specified transition cell can instead give an exact count, as it does for the twenty eligible A-to-A control keys at their chosen renderings.

These bounds also show why inverse weighting by a raw class frequency does not solve the problem by itself. The selection included six fixed anchors and deterministic rank and balancing rules; no probability of sampling from a specified natural task population has been supplied. Within a transition class there can still be strong heterogeneity by residues, number of lifts, presentation or query size. Multiplying a model's measured accuracy in a class by the class's weight in the larger pool constructs a descriptive standardized score only under a within-class transport assumption. The arithmetic oracle and constant baselines have a useful property here. Their behavior is known on all keys, so their census values need no extrapolation.

\subsection{Relationship to selective prediction and behavioral testing}
Selective classification treats permission to decline as part of prediction and studies how attainable error varies with coverage. Geifman and El-Yaniv give a procedure for choosing a selective classifier to meet a risk target under their evaluation setting \cite{selective}. Our rule instead fixes the epistemic meaning of abstention on each individual record. This lets us ask where a system withheld a claim that was mathematically available, where it spoke despite a concrete counterexample, and what the best sound decoder of a given writer could achieve. The two settings meet in the need to measure coverage explicitly. A bound on accepted prediction risk under one distribution and a record-specific universal guarantee remain different claims.

The intervention also belongs to the tradition of behavioral testing described by Ribeiro and colleagues \cite{checklist}. Changing a cutoff may preserve the required action or force it to change. Here both possibilities can be generated from arithmetic and checked without annotator disagreement. The useful relation to benchmark design is the ability to nominate a tempting alternative policy and select cases where it makes a different prediction from the specification. When the policies make identical predictions, no score can distinguish them. Deliberate conflict enrichment can be scientifically informative precisely because it is unlike the underlying frequency distribution. The paper therefore reports both.

\section{Experiments that would resolve the remaining ambiguities}\label{sec:next}
\subsection{Change one instruction on matched records}
Freeze fresh keys before inspecting outputs and administer both the linear and quadratic instructions to each reader for the same record and query. For every observation register the pair of oracle actions and the two directed predictions made by an old-rule policy. Use independent contexts, randomize which instruction an arm receives first, and counterbalance packet neighbors across treatments. The primary unit is a record with two valid public outputs. Report the number of records that switch when required, remain stable when required, change unnecessarily, and lack a usable comparison. This makes a model's counterfactual response under the other instruction directly observable for the same item.

An especially informative rejection criterion would require failure on an actual old-to-new switch where the model succeeded on the same record under the old instruction. This removes one ambiguity of the present study, in which a wrong old-matching sign could have been an error under either instruction if the model never learned the original action. It cannot remove all item-level confounding because mathematical features may imply the old action. The design should publish the attainable cells of its control table before setting any quota. If a proposed comparison asks a fixed record to have two old oracle labels, the design is impossible. One can change how the rule is presented across contexts or change the codebook; those are different interventions and should be declared.

\subsection{Separate formatting from deciding}
On a prespecified subset of ambiguous, positive and negative queries, cross packet size one versus eight with two allowed output budgets. Compare public usability, licensed answers among usable responses and licensed answers per assigned row. A one-question response can localize a format failure to one row, but it also changes context length and batching. The full factorial comparison makes those changes observable. Keep one response per registered cell in the primary record and account for the full number of generations in advance. Fix a secondary parsing policy for explanations only if the research question includes whether a human can recover an action from free text.

Such a test could falsify the interpretation that the operational failure of Flash High primarily reflects an insufficient public-output budget. If its public validity stayed low even with a larger budget under identical instructions or if its valid decisions were substantially weaker on comparable records, that account would need revision. Conversely, a larger budget that restored public arrays would still leave the quality of their decisions to be measured. Treating a valid output as a semantic pass would simply substitute one measurement failure for another.

\subsection{Separate algebraic transfer from presentation invariance}
Use the derivative criterion to freeze a third polynomial, for example $S^2+S$, with cases that exchange the flat residue class. Balance support types where the writer permits it. Pair a flat singleton and a split singleton with the same observation width and comparable weights, and include clear negatives at both moduli. Decide whether every issued guess must again carry an independently generated yes/no witness pair. An experiment of this kind would make a specific square-only cue predict an error on an identified cell. The analytic argument already gives the new oracle; the unknowns are the model outputs.

Independently, rotate each eight-row packet through its eight positions while retaining membership and ordering up to cyclic rotation. Make the outcome the proportion of individual records for which the response changes somewhere across presentations. If the budget only supports a subset, freeze its selection first and disclose the scope. Crossing a change of position with equivalent-cutoff renderings would distinguish some kinds of context sensitivity from sensitivity to a mathematically redundant number. A clean answer to either question is more valuable than a larger undifferentiated score on another collection of easy negatives.

\section{Conclusion}
The sequence began with a concrete defect in truth-only evaluation. An earlier reader could answer almost every hidden question accurately and still assert a sign on every ambiguous held-out observation. A support oracle and an exact writer made the defect measurable. The new linear benchmark showed several empirical ways to fall short of that oracle, including missed positives, unsupported assertions, unnecessary abstention and failure to emit a usable answer. Returning all settled signs did not guarantee the ability to withhold a claim on incomplete evidence. An explicit yes or no could coincide with the likely hidden outcome and still admit an immediate counterexample.

The quadratic experiment then produced a genuine positive result. Under the registered settings one reader matched every licensed action on 240 old-rule conflicts and 64 agreement controls. The other did so on 79 conflicts and 38 controls, with concrete witnesses for every one of its 68 unsupported assertions. A default N matched more old conflict actions than the second reader did. This limits an attractive explanation for its mistakes while leaving the mistakes themselves fully checkable. The strongest general contribution of the series is a measurement design that specifies what survived storage, reconstructs what can be asserted from it, and tests a prospective change on which rival policies diverge. Under those conditions both a perfect score and an error can teach us something precise.

\section{Protocol ledger, budgets and offline reproduction}\label{sec:replay}
The release contains three immutable study ZIPs and one small antecedent evidence directory. The local and hosted linear experiments used the same packet set. The hosted ZIP includes the thinking-off supplement. The transfer ZIP contains the final completed run and the original frozen linear panel needed to verify disjointness. Interim transfer snapshots were partial records of the same dispatches. They contribute no second sample. The evidence below distinguishes a registered study from a later analysis of its outputs \cite{runs}.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular*}{\linewidth}{@{\extracolsep{\fill}}llll@{}}\toprule
Study & Protocol & Amendment & Result\\\midrule
Local linear &\texttt{38b5d30} &\texttt{92d010b} &\texttt{4aaddf1}\\
Hosted linear &\texttt{d4c2333} &\texttt{2dd5d49} &\texttt{2f7672f}\\
Quadratic &\texttt{65e04c6} & --- &\texttt{55cc4b7}\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular*}
\end{center}
The local scoring snapshot \texttt{12876a4} preceded the Qwen amendment. Git bundles in the original ZIPs preserve the study histories. The archives and their SHA-256 hashes establish the byte identity of the data used in this reanalysis. Local commit times establish the recorded order on this workstation. No third-party notarization of the freeze time or provider-signed attestation is claimed.

\begin{table}[htbp]\centering\small
\caption{Dispatch and usability accounting. A request is one eight-question packet. Each hosted estimate uses the registered per-token tariffs, with DeepSeek at peak uncached rates.}\label{tab:resource}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrr@{}}\toprule
Study & Configuration & Dispatched & Usable & USD upper\\\midrule
Local & Llama 3.2 &38&38&0\\
& Qwen2.5 &38&38&0\\

Across the three protocols there were nine study-by-configuration assignments and 2,736 scheduled row slots. Of these, $308\cdot8=2,464$ were dispatched. The 272 unsent slots all belong to the local R1 stop. Of the dispatched slots, $267\cdot8=2,136$ came from valid packets and 328 from 41 invalid packets. These units count the calibration rows in the linear panels and the repeated presentation of a panel to different readers. They are intended for reconciling receipts, not for pooling accuracy.

The two hosted budgets were separate, each with a cap of USD 1.25. The linear run including its supplement had a full reservation of USD 1.128448 and a usage-derived upper amount of USD 0.1475546. The transfer reserved USD 1.011712 and used at most USD 0.1409366 at the recorded tariffs. Their sum in Table~\ref{tab:resource} is an accounting total over 190 hosted requests in two studies. No invoice was inspected. Local electricity, hardware and research labor fall outside this amount. Reproducing the scored tables from public receipts requires no new inference.

\subsection{How to reproduce the numbers without either provider}
Unpack the outer ZIP and open its top-level directory. The two commands below run the finite historical checks and the cross-study replay, respectively. The latter should write new files to a separate directory so the archived comparison remains available.
\begin{verbatim}
python3 -B evidence/antecedents/replay.py
python3 -B audit/replay.py evidence --output replayed
\end{verbatim}
The first command needs Python and Git. It reconstructs the pinned fixture with a small Git object pack, executes the historical programs and checks six result files against their archived bytes. The second needs only the Python standard library. It authenticates the original study ZIPs by digest, verifies the 76 distinct prompt texts against their frozen digests, parses the public responses and reconciles them with dispatch journals. It reconstructs compatible integers and their sign counts for the displayed records, regenerates the transfer design census from the writers, and reconciles price arithmetic with token usage. The output includes a decision row for each scheduled slot, including missing and unrun slots.

A reader auditing a claimed guess can start in \texttt{audit/decisions.csv}. Filter to the desired family, model and a target of A with a returned Y or N. The yes and no witness columns identify two integers compatible with the displayed record. Verify their membership by division by 81, then test them modulo the displayed $m$ using either $S$ or $S^2$ as appropriate. On a settled row the absence of an opposite witness cannot be established by checking one representative; enumerate the printed list and its three lifts or use the formula proved above. Inspect \texttt{scores.csv} for action-by-action tables, \texttt{semantic\_repeats.csv} for the equivalence-key audit, and \texttt{census.csv} for all nine old/new transitions in both modulus bands.

The single LuaLaTeX source embeds the actual Nous PNG and the three vector figures. In a fresh writable directory run LuaLaTeX twice on the source to resolve cross-references and the table of contents. Recreating the plots is optional for rebuilding the manuscript. The figure script reads the exported audit tables and uses Matplotlib. No external chapter or bibliography files are required.

\section{A compact decision audit}\label{sec:quick}
Consider any published observation. Begin with the stated hidden domain and expand every low residue into its three compatible integers. With the linear rule and $R=\{80\}$, $e=80$, $Z=80$, every candidate $80,161,242$ agrees modulo 81. The same list at $Z=81$ has one positive and two negatives modulo 243. A change from Y to A is compelled even though the tag, low residue and weight have not moved. If the second query instead used $e=79$ the answer would be N in both bands. Losing precision in the stored record is relative to the query being asked.

For a quadratic example with multiple low residues, take the actual row $R=\{12,13,14\}$, $Z=79$, $e=63$. Modulo 81 the three low squares are 63, 7 and 34. Every lift of 12 agrees and no lift of 13 or 14 agrees. A is required, with $S=12$ and $S=13$ as the shortest certificate. The observed negative from Flash off is refuted by the former. For an opposite-direction example, the transfer ledger includes $R=\{65,\ldots,72\}$, $Z=218$, $e=144$. Integers 69 and 65 have square remainders 144 and 94 modulo 243. Flash off answered Y; 65 provides the counterexample. In each case the certificate establishes precisely why the action exceeded the information in the record.

Finally one can audit a claim about universal support even without selecting a hidden state. At $Z=82$ set $R=\{9,36,63\}$ and $e=81$. The three low squares are $81,1{,}296,3{,}969$, all congruent to 81 modulo 243. Each low residue is divisible by three, so Lemma~\ref{lem:lift} propagates its value to all three lifts. All nine histories give yes. A multivalued list can therefore support a positive answer even above the precision boundary. Cases of this kind complete the logic of the benchmark. A reader has to recognize both the existence of genuine ambiguity and the situations in which apparently missing detail makes no difference.
